Bethlem myopathy

Mendelian MONDO:0008029 Pathograph 67 Show in embeddings browser Collagen 6-related myopathy Congenital muscular dystrophy

Bethlem myopathy is the usually milder, adult-ambulant end of the collagen VI-related dystrophy spectrum. Pathogenic COL6A1, COL6A2 or COL6A3 variants act through dominant or less commonly recessive mechanisms affecting collagen VI assembly, secretion or extracellular organization. Interstitial fibroblast-derived matrix dysfunction is linked to myofibre injury; autophagy, mitochondrial permeability and TGF-beta abnormalities are supported mainly by experimental models and selected patient studies, with incompletely defined intermediates. Slowly progressive proximal weakness and finger-flexor, elbow and Achilles contractures are characteristic, but onset and contractures vary and respiratory weakness can develop while walking is retained. Keloids, atrophic scars and follicular hyperkeratosis are useful connective-tissue clues. The historical label benign does not capture the possible adult functional decline.

Definitions

1
GeneReviews Bethlem muscular dystrophy definition
Bethlem muscular dystrophy is the mild end of the collagen VI-related dystrophy continuum and is characterized by the combination of proximal muscle weakness and joint contractures.
OTHER

The GeneReviews chapter states the defining clinical combination for Bethlem muscular dystrophy.
PMID:15689448 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"BM is a relatively mild dominantly inherited disorder with proximal weakness and distal joint contractures."
A cohort sequencing study frames Bethlem myopathy as the mild, dominantly inherited COL6 disorder with proximal weakness and distal contractures.
👪

Inheritance

2
Autosomal dominant inheritance HP:0000006
Bethlem myopathy is usually inherited in an autosomal dominant manner, typically through dominant-negative COL6A1, COL6A2, or COL6A3 variants that are incorporated into collagen VI monomers.
Autosomal dominant inheritance
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:20301676 SUPPORT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"Bethlem muscular dystrophy is usually inherited in an autosomal dominant manner"
GeneReviews records autosomal dominant inheritance as the usual mode for Bethlem muscular dystrophy.
PMID:8782832 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"A distinctive feature of this autosomal dominant disorder consists of contractures of multiple joints in addition to generalized muscular weakness and wasting."
The original gene-identification study describes Bethlem myopathy as an autosomal dominant disorder.
Autosomal recessive inheritance HP:0000007
Recessive Bethlem myopathy occurs with biallelic COL6 variants and residual-function mechanisms. COL6A2-related myosclerosis overlaps the spectrum but is clinically distinguishable by severe stiffness and restricted joint movement; it is not synonymous with all recessive Bethlem disease.

Discussions and Knowledge Gaps

2
Does the Col6a1-null mouse, which lacks collagen VI entirely, faithfully model dominant-negative Bethlem myopathy, in which an abnormal collagen VI network is still present in the matrix?
HUMAN MODEL MISMATCH OPEN disc_bethlem_col6a1_null_model_fidelity
Attached to
pathophysiology#Myofibre mitochondrial dysfunction and permeability transition pore dysregulation pathophysiology#Defective autophagic clearance of damaged organelles
Complete Col6a1-null mice reproduce selected mitochondrial and autophagy defects but differ from human dominant structural variants. Many dominant Bethlem muscle biopsies have normal collagen VI localization as well as preserved signal. Neither normal staining nor a null model resolves the exact molecular dysfunction in every patient. Genotype-stratified functional assays are needed to assess transferability of therapeutic mechanisms.
Proposed experiments
Permeability transition and autophagic flux by COL6 genotype class
exp_bethlem_dominant_negative_ptp_flux
Measure permeability transition pore sensitivity and autophagic flux in myofibres or myoblasts from genotyped patients carrying dominant-negative COL6A1 exon 14 skipping, and compare against recessive collagen VI null patients and unaffected controls.
Bethlem dominant-negative knock-in mouse
exp_bethlem_knockin_mouse
Generate a knock-in mouse carrying a Bethlem dominant-negative Col6a1 in-frame exon deletion and compare its mitochondrial, autophagic, and contracture phenotype with the existing Col6a1-null mouse.
Posed 2026-07-31T00:00:00Z
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:21691338 SUPPORT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"In contrast to the human disease, these mice only show a mild neuromuscular disorder without much overt weakness."
The review states directly that the Col6a1-null mouse phenotype is milder than human disease, which is the translational mismatch at issue.
PMID:37569848 SUPPORT DIRECT Human Clinical
"Muscle biopsies from 13 out of 15 BM patients with dominant mutations exhibited a normal collagen VI pattern, as evidenced by the continuous overlap with the anti-perlecan staining (Table 2)."
Normal abundance and basal-lamina colocalization in most dominant Bethlem biopsies in this series; a normal result does not exclude disease.
Which molecular partner mediates the interaction of collagen VI with the muscle basement membrane and links the microfibrillar network to the sarcolemma?
KNOWLEDGE GAP OPEN disc_bethlem_matrix_sarcolemma_receptor
Attached to
pathophysiology#Impaired matrix-to-sarcolemma anchoring of the myofibre
The matrix-to-myofibre link remains incompletely resolved. The 2025 Col6a2-null study identified collagen VI-associated proteins and altered TGF-beta-containing complexes, but co-immunoprecipitation does not prove direct pairwise binding or identify a universal receptor pathway in human Bethlem disease. Whether altered mechanical coupling, extracellular growth-factor availability and mitochondrial/autophagy pathways act in sequence or partly in parallel remains unresolved.
Proposed experiments
Muscle basement membrane collagen VI interactome
exp_bethlem_basement_membrane_interactome
Perform proximity labeling or crosslinking mass spectrometry on the muscle basement membrane to identify the collagen VI binding partners present at the myofibre surface.
Biglycan and dystroglycan complex phenocopy test
exp_bethlem_biglycan_dgc_phenocopy
Test whether disruption of biglycan or of the dystrophin-associated protein complex phenocopies the collagen VI mitochondrial defect in otherwise wild-type myofibres.
Posed 2026-07-31T00:00:00Z

⚙

Pathophysiology

15
Collagen VI alpha-chain gene defects
Pathogenic COL6A1, COL6A2 or COL6A3 variants alter the obligatory collagen VI chains. Dominant structural alleles and recessive residual-function combinations can cause Bethlem myopathy. Their consequences depend on the affected domain and assembly step: entry into a monomer does not imply that mutant protein reaches tetramers or the extracellular matrix. Ordinary heterozygous loss-of-function alleles can be clinically tolerated; pathogenic structural alleles should not be equated with simple gene dosage reduction.

Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:15689448 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"INTRODUCTION: Mutations in the genes encoding collagen VI (COL6A1, COL6A2, and COL6A3) cause Bethlem myopathy (BM) and Ullrich congenital muscular dystrophy (UCMD)."
Establishes the three collagen VI genes as the genetic cause of Bethlem myopathy.
PMID:8782832 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Both mutations disrupt the Gly-X-Y motif of the triple helical domain by substitution of Gly for either Val or Ser."
Identifies the triple-helix-disrupting substitutions that constitute the primary molecular lesion.
PMID:10419498 SUPPORT DIRECT In Vitro
"The mutant alpha1(VI) chains associated intracellularly with alpha2(VI) and alpha3(VI) to form disulfide-bonded monomers, but further assembly into dimers and tetramers was prevented, and molecules containing the mutant chain were not secreted."
Direct assembly/secretion study of one patient exon-14-skipping allele, not a universal mechanism for all COL6 variants.
Defective intracellular collagen VI assembly
Variant-specific defects impair intracellular collagen VI oligomerization. For the studied COL6A1 exon-14-skipping allele, mutant chains enter heterotrimeric monomers but fail to form dimers and tetramers. Other assembly-competent dominant alleles may instead interfere with later extracellular organization; the one-in-sixteen normal-tetramer argument is not applicable to every Bethlem allele.
Experimental models

Downstream
Impaired collagen VI secretion Failure to assemble export-competent oligomers prevents secretion of mutant-containing molecules.

PMID:10419498 SUPPORT DIRECT In Vitro
"The mutant alpha1(VI) chains associated intracellularly with alpha2(VI) and alpha3(VI) to form disulfide-bonded monomers, but further assembly into dimers and tetramers was prevented, and molecules containing the mutant chain were not secreted."
Direct assembly/secretion study of one patient exon-14-skipping allele, not a universal mechanism for all COL6 variants.
Impaired collagen VI secretion
Some Bethlem alleles cause intracellular retention and reduced extracellular delivery of collagen VI. This is demonstrated for one COL6A1 exon-14-skipping allele and in fibroblasts from a recessive COL6A2 proband, rather than an obligatory defect in every patient.

PMID:10419498 SUPPORT DIRECT In Vitro
"The mutant alpha1(VI) chains associated intracellularly with alpha2(VI) and alpha3(VI) to form disulfide-bonded monomers, but further assembly into dimers and tetramers was prevented, and molecules containing the mutant chain were not secreted."

PMID:40550965 SUPPORT DIRECT In Vitro
"The proband’s cells (III:2) presented the absence of secreted protein and collagen VI network; collagen staining was observable only in the cell’s cytoplasm as in NT well (63X)."
Patient fibroblast phenotype in a recessive COL6A2 family; individual variant effects were not isolated.
Endomysial collagen VI network dysfunction
Collagen VI is supplied to muscle by interstitial fibroblasts. Variant-dependent defects affect extracellular matrix organization or function, but routine muscle staining can be normal in Bethlem disease, including preserved colocalization with basal-lamina perlecan in 13 of 15 dominant cases in a 2023 series. Normal staining does not prove normal molecular interactions; conversely, basement-membrane mislocalization is not a universal Bethlem finding.

Downstream
Impaired matrix-to-sarcolemma anchoring of the myofibre Loss of the functional collagen VI network at the muscle basement membrane removes the microfibrillar bridge between the myofibre surface and the surrounding matrix.
Tendon and periarticular connective tissue involvement Collagen VI is also a pericellular matrix component of tendon, so the same network defect alters tendon and periarticular connective tissue.
Dermal extracellular matrix dysfunction The same collagen VI network defect is expressed in skin, producing the characteristic follicular and scarring phenotype.
Defective autophagic clearance of damaged organelles Extracellular collagen VI deficiency is linked to defective autophagy in knockout models, with unresolved matrix-to-intracellular intermediates.
Dysregulated extracellular TGF-beta bioavailability Loss of collagen VI changes extracellular TGF-beta availability and protein associations in Col6a2-null models.
Neonatal hypotonia Early matrix-muscle dysfunction may produce congenital weakness before the later progressive degenerative course; the intervening developmental mechanisms remain incompletely resolved.
Decreased fetal movement Early matrix-muscle dysfunction may produce congenital weakness before the later progressive degenerative course; the intervening developmental mechanisms remain incompletely resolved.

"In muscle, the cell of origin for collagen VI is the interstitial fibroblast."
Review of the cellular source of collagen VI.
Impaired matrix-to-sarcolemma anchoring of the myofibre
Abnormal collagen VI interactions may compromise matrix-to-cell coupling and force transmission. Biglycan and the dystrophin-associated complex were proposed partners in the earlier review, but the specific interaction defect is not demonstrated in every Bethlem patient. Human ultrastructural abnormalities and mouse mechanical measurements support matrix dysfunction without establishing a universal receptor-to-mitochondria signaling chain.

Cell-culture data support reduced matrix adherence, although the precise myofibre receptor remains unidentified.
+ 1 more reference
Myofibre mitochondrial dysfunction and permeability transition pore dysregulation
Col6a1-null mouse myofibres have abnormal mitochondrial and sarcoplasmic-reticulum ultrastructure and latent permeability-transition susceptibility. Cyclosporin A or genetic cyclophilin-D ablation rescues experimental abnormalities. Muscle biopsies in a short open human pilot also showed mitochondrial dysfunction and increased apoptosis. These selected COL6-disease observations do not establish identical abnormalities in every Bethlem patient or clinical benefit from pore modulation.

+ 1 more reference
Defective autophagic clearance of damaged organelles
Col6a1-null mouse muscle has impaired autophagic flux, inadequate autophagosome induction and persistence of damaged organelles. Human Bethlem/UCMD biopsies show reduced beclin-1 and BNIP3 abundance, consistent with this pathway but not a direct measurement of human autophagic flux. Experimental activation of autophagy improves mouse myofibre survival.

Myofibre apoptosis
Increased myofibre apoptosis is a measured downstream injury process in collagen VI-deficient mouse muscle and selected human biopsies. Reducing apoptosis is an experimental outcome and does not by itself establish improved patient function.

PMID:14625552 SUPPORT Model Organism
"Col6a1-/- muscles have a loss of contractile strength associated with ultrastructural alterations of sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) and mitochondria and spontaneous apoptosis."
Documents spontaneous myofibre apoptosis alongside loss of contractile strength.
PMID:19293339 SUPPORT Model Organism
"Mice lacking ColVI (Col6a1(-/-)) display a myopathic phenotype associated with ultrastructural alterations of mitochondria and sarcoplasmic reticulum, mitochondrial dysfunction with abnormal opening of the permeability transition pore (PTP) and increased apoptosis of muscle fibers."
Links pore opening to increased apoptosis of muscle fibres.
Progressive myofibre degeneration
Myofibre injury and loss produce progressive proximal-predominant weakness and variable enzyme leakage and myopathic electromyography. Functional decline can occur over decades, and wheelchair use for longer distances is distinct from complete loss of independent walking. Fibrotic replacement is a related, separately represented tissue process.

Endomysial fibrosis
Interstitial collagen accumulation accompanies chronic dystrophic muscle remodeling. It can contribute to stiffness and impaired force transmission, but mouse weakness precedes overt fibrosis, so fibrosis is not assumed to explain all early weakness.

PMID:21691338 SUPPORT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"In samples from patients in more-advanced stages of the disease, overtly dystrophic features are evident, including rare degenerating and regenerating fibers and a prominent build-up of interstitial fibrous tissue in the muscle."
Describes the dystrophic and fibrotic muscle pathology of the collagen VI-related myopathies.
Tendon and periarticular connective tissue involvement
Collagen VI-associated connective-tissue dysfunction accompanies early distal laxity and later contractures. The long finger-flexor, elbow and Achilles pattern is characteristic, but human tendon pathology has not established a single mechanism for every contracture. On wrist dorsiflexion, finger-flexor shortening can prevent full finger extension. Some recessive cases have little or no contracture despite substantial weakness.

Documents the early-childhood distal hyperlaxity that later converts to contracture.
Dermal extracellular matrix dysfunction
Collagen VI dysfunction affects dermal matrix organization and is associated with follicular hyperkeratosis, soft skin and abnormal scarring. The detailed route to each skin manifestation remains uncertain. Col6a1-null mice have mechanically weaker skin and altered wound ultrastructure but do not reproduce the human keloid phenotype or an overt wound-healing delay.

Abnormal cutaneous scarring
Keloid or atrophic scarring is a clinical skin-repair manifestation of collagen VI disease. Its occurrence is variable, and the null mouse does not recreate the full human scar phenotype.

Describes the characteristic collagen VI skin phenotype.
Restrictive respiratory involvement
Respiratory muscle weakness can reduce vital capacity and cause nocturnal hypoventilation even while patients remain ambulant. Severity is variable: 13 of 22 tested Bethlem patients in one cohort had FVC below 80% predicted, and two began noninvasive ventilation at ages 24 and 49. These clinic-based observations do not establish a population prevalence.

Dysregulated extracellular TGF-beta bioavailability
Col6a2-null mice have excessive resting TGF-beta activity, but a blunted early release after injury and relatively prolonged later activity. Protein-complex and fibroblast reporter assays implicate altered extracellular sequestration or activation without increased total ligand transcription or protein. This is a COL6 model mechanism, not a demonstrated universal Bethlem patient defect. Disrupting Ltbp4-TGF-beta binding did not rescue the mouse phenotype.

Histopathology

1
Mild dystrophic changes with interstitial fibrosis
Muscle biopsy in advanced disease shows overtly dystrophic features with rare degenerating and regenerating fibres and a prominent build-up of interstitial fibrous tissue; early biopsies may show only fibre atrophy or fibre type disproportion.
Show evidence (2 references)

Describes the muscle biopsy pattern of the collagen VI-related myopathies.

"Muscle biopsy in the father showed dystrophic changes with normal immmunostaining for collagen VI, sarcoglycans, and dysferlin."
A Bethlem case series documents dystrophic muscle histology.

Genetic Associations

3
COL6A1 (Causative)
Gene: COL6A1 hgnc:2211 gene page HUGO Gene Nomenclature Committee (hgnc) Relation: this disease-associated gene is this gene This disease-associated gene is COL6A1 (hgnc:2211). hgnc:2211 is a gene from the HUGO Gene Nomenclature Committee. relationship_type: CAUSATIVE variant_origin: GERMLINE
Show evidence (4 references)
PMID:15689448 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"INTRODUCTION: Mutations in the genes encoding collagen VI (COL6A1, COL6A2, and COL6A3) cause Bethlem myopathy (BM) and Ullrich congenital muscular dystrophy (UCMD)."
Sequencing evidence establishes the three collagen VI genes as Bethlem myopathy causes.
PMID:8782832 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Sequence analysis in four families reveals a mutation in COL6A1 in one and a COL6A2 mutation in two other kindreds."
The original study identified a COL6A1 mutation segregating in a Bethlem kindred.
PMID:10419498 SUPPORT DIRECT In Vitro
"The mutant alpha1(VI) chains associated intracellularly with alpha2(VI) and alpha3(VI) to form disulfide-bonded monomers, but further assembly into dimers and tetramers was prevented, and molecules containing the mutant chain were not secreted."

Physical and occupational therapy for contracture management
Action: Physical TherapyNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is Physical Therapy (NCIT:C15302). NCIT:C15302 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. NCIT:C15302
Platform: Behavioral / lifestyle
Physical therapy and occupational therapy provide recommendations for joint stretching, swimming, and aquatherapy. Contracture management is the central supportive intervention in Bethlem myopathy, because contracture progression causes disability in its own right even when weakness progresses only slowly.

Specialist care can include serial casting and selected Achilles tendon release followed by casting when severe or asymmetric shortening impairs gait. Surgery disrupting other joint capsules is generally discouraged because fixed postoperative contractures and loss of motion can result. Nocturnal noninvasive ventilation

Respiratory support is guided by upright/supine pulmonary testing and polysomnography, including carbon-dioxide monitoring. Respiratory, cardiac, and orthopedic surveillance
Action: supportive careNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is supportive care (NCIT:C15747). NCIT:C15747 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Supportive Care NCIT:C15747
At least annual upright and supine FVC, sleep evaluation for hypoventilation, clinical/radiographic scoliosis assessment and rehabilitation review are recommended for Bethlem disease. Initial pulmonary testing begins in childhood, around age five when feasible. GeneReviews recommends annual ECG/echocardiography to assess secondary right-heart strain from inadequate ventilation; this does not imply primary cardiomyopathy is a characteristic Bethlem feature. Mobility-device needs and joint function should be reassessed over time.

Genetic counseling
Action: Genetic CounselingNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is Genetic Counseling (NCIT:C15240). NCIT:C15240 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. NCIT:C15240
A person with an autosomal dominant pathogenic variant has a 50% transmission risk in each pregnancy; sibling risk is 50% when a parent is heterozygous. Parental somatic/germline mosaicism can complicate apparently de novo families, and clinical severity varies within families. For recessive disease with two carrier parents, each pregnancy has a 25% affected, 50% carrier and 25% noncarrier chance. Once familial variants are established, prenatal and preimplantation genetic testing are possible.
Show evidence (5 references)
PMID:20301676 SUPPORT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"Parental somatic mosaicism (and concomitant germline mosaicism) is not uncommon in the autosomal dominant COL6-RDs."
GeneReviews highlights parental mosaicism as a counseling consideration.
PMID:20301676 SUPPORT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"The severity of COL6-RD manifestations may vary among family members who are heterozygous for the same pathogenic variant."
GeneReviews notes intrafamilial variability, a key counseling point.
PMID:20301676 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"If a parent of the proband has the pathogenic variant identified in the proband and/or is affected, the risk to the sibs of inheriting the variant is 50%. The severity of COL6-RD manifestations may vary among family members who are heterozygous for the same pathogenic variant."

Cyclophilin D and permeability transition pore modulation
Action: PharmacotherapyNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is Pharmacotherapy (NCIT:C15986). NCIT:C15986 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. NCIT:C15986
Agent: cyclosporin A CHEBI:4031 Chemical Entities of Biological Interest (CHEBI) Relation: this treatment uses this therapeutic agent This treatment uses cyclosporin A (CHEBI:4031). CHEBI:4031 is a therapeutic agent from Chemical Entities of Biological Interest.
Platform: Small molecule
Cyclosporin A is investigational for COL6 myopathy. A one-month open pilot in five patients (four UCMD and one Bethlem) showed improved mitochondrial and apoptotic biopsy readouts, without establishing functional benefit or long-term Bethlem efficacy. Genetic cyclophilin-D ablation supports the target in Col6a1-null mice. Immunosuppression and toxicity preclude treating a biopsy response as a routine-care recommendation.
Mechanism Target:
INHIBITS Myofibre mitochondrial dysfunction and permeability transition pore dysregulation — Investigational cyclophilin-D-dependent pore desensitization can improve mitochondrial biopsy readouts; functional and long-term benefit in Bethlem disease remains unproved.

Investigational low-protein diet for autophagy modulation
Action: Therapeutic Dietary InterventionNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is Therapeutic Dietary Intervention (NCIT:C64892). NCIT:C64892 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. NCIT:C64892
A one-year open, uncontrolled pilot enrolled eight adults and analyzed seven completers (six Bethlem, one UCMD). Muscle autophagy-associated markers increased and apoptosis decreased, but human flux was not directly quantified. The six ambulant Bethlem patients improved on the four-stair test; six-minute walk, ten-meter walk, strength and the mixed-group FVC change were not statistically significant. Participants lost about 9% body weight and 7% lean mass despite preservation of the lean-to-fat percentage. The diet remains investigational and requires nutritional oversight; the study does not establish long-term clinical efficacy.
Mechanism Target:
MODULATES Defective autophagic clearance of damaged organelles — Human muscle autophagy-associated markers increase during the exploratory diet intervention. Experimental mouse autophagy rescue motivates the approach, but a restoration of human flux or sustained clinical efficacy has not been established.

Markers in seven completers; increased abundance does not directly measure human autophagic flux.

Individualized moderate aerobic exercise
Action: Exercise InterventionNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is Exercise Intervention (NCIT:C62739). NCIT:C62739 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. NCIT:C62739
Supervised individualized moderate exercise can complement stretching and mobility support. In a ten-week home-training study, four men improved peak oxygen uptake by 16% compared with baseline, while two controls did not improve. CK remained stable and functional tests did not change significantly. The small study supports feasibility and aerobic conditioning, without establishing long-term disease modification or safety of intense eccentric loading.

Diagnosis

4
Muscle collagen VI immunohistochemistry
Collagen VI abundance and colocalization with a basement-membrane marker can be normal in Bethlem disease: 13 of 15 dominant cases in one series retained a normal perlecan-overlapping pattern. Thus a normal stain does not exclude the diagnosis. Abnormal staining can support COL6 disease but is not a universal requirement, and expression may vary with age.
diagnostic procedure NCIT:C18020 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
Results: Retained but mislocalized collagen VI, away from the basement membrane, supports dominant collagen VI disease.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:37569848 SUPPORT DIRECT Human Clinical
"Muscle biopsies from 13 out of 15 BM patients with dominant mutations exhibited a normal collagen VI pattern, as evidenced by the continuous overlap with the anti-perlecan staining (Table 2)."
Normal abundance and basal-lamina colocalization in most dominant Bethlem biopsies in this series; a normal result does not exclude disease.
COL6A1, COL6A2, and COL6A3 molecular genetic testing
Diagnosis requires compatible clinical findings and pathogenic or likely pathogenic COL6A1, COL6A2 or COL6A3 variant(s), with inheritance and segregation assessed. Testing can use a multigene panel or exome/genome sequencing and selected deletion/duplication or RNA studies when standard sequencing is unrevealing. The 62% putative-variant yield in a 2005 coding-exon study is historical and should not be presented as modern sensitivity. Variants of uncertain significance neither confirm nor exclude disease; functional evidence must be interpreted with appropriate controls. Deep-intronic COL6A1 testing is relevant to the wider COL6 diagnostic spectrum, without labeling its typical severe phenotype as Bethlem.
genetic testing NCIT:C15709 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
Results: A heterozygous or biallelic COL6A1, COL6A2, or COL6A3 pathogenic variant confirms the diagnosis.
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:20301676 SUPPORT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"The diagnosis can be confirmed by identification of a heterozygous or biallelic pathogenic variant(s) in COL6A1, COL6A2, or COL6A3."
GeneReviews defines the molecular confirmation criterion.
PMID:15689448 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"We found putative mutations in one of the COL6 genes in 62% of patients."
Quantifies the diagnostic yield of systematic COL6 sequencing.
url:https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sites/books/NBK1503/?report=reader SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"The identification of variant(s) of uncertain significance cannot be used to confirm or rule out the diagnosis."
GeneReviews explicitly limits interpretation of VUS.
Muscle imaging
MRI may show peripheral involvement of vastus lateralis with central rectus-femoris involvement, supporting a COL6 phenotype and helping select diagnostic testing. Imaging is supportive rather than perfectly specific; overlapping patterns occur in calpainopathy, and some mild Bethlem patients have normal MRI.
diagnostic procedure NCIT:C18020 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
Results: A concentric outer-to-inner pattern with a rectus femoris central cloud supports collagen VI-related myopathy.
Show evidence (3 references)

"This analysis, therefore, is also a useful diagnostic tool (Figure 2).30,32–34 MRI of muscle can be very useful in the recognition of a collagen VI-related myopathy and in the differentiation of collagen VI-related myopathies from LMNA-related Emery–Dreifuss muscular dystrophy, as well as from..."
Describes the concentric muscle imaging signature.

Cultured dermal fibroblast collagen VI analysis
Cultured fibroblast immunocytochemistry can reveal disorganized, stippled, sparse or intracellularly retained collagen VI and complement molecular interpretation. The assay compartment differs from muscle immunohistochemistry. A 2025 recessive family showed absent extracellular network in the proband and preserved networks in single-variant relatives; this does not independently classify either allele in isolation.

Progression

3
Variable onset from birth to adulthood
Age: Prenatal life to adulthood
Onset is variable. The 1999 seven-kindred series found manifestations in nearly all affected children by age two, whereas the 2023 cohort included 10 of 33 with symptoms at birth, 11 in early childhood, two in adolescence, six first noticing difficulty in early adulthood and four initially asymptomatic. These ascertainment-specific distributions are not contradictory universal age cutoffs.
Show evidence (4 references)
PMID:10219778 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Our analysis shows that nearly all children exhibit weakness or contractures during the first 2 years of life."
Natural-history data in seven kindreds establish onset within the first two years.
PMID:10219778 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Early features include diminished foetal movements, neonatal hypotonia and congenital contractures which are of a dynamic nature during childhood."
The same cohort documents the early prenatal and neonatal features.
PMID:37569848 SUPPORT DIRECT Human Clinical
"Ten patients with BM (30%) exhibited symptoms at birth, with a varying combination of congenital hip dislocation (CHD) in four, hypotonia in four, club foot in four, torticollis in three, and contractures of the elbow and knee in one."
Ten of 33 Bethlem patients; individual congenital findings each have their own count.
+ 1 more reference
Contracture development in later childhood and adolescence
Age: Late first decade to teenage years
Achilles tendon and elbow contractures typically emerge late in the first decade and during the teenage years and then extend to the long finger flexors and shoulders, sometimes producing spinal rigidity.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:21691338 SUPPORT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"In Bethlem myopathy, typical contractures of the Achilles tendons (Figure 1d) and elbows tend to develop late in the first decade of life and during teenage years."
The review dates the onset of the characteristic Bethlem contractures.
PMID:21691338 SUPPORT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"These contractures progress to involve the long finger flexors and the shoulders; they can also affect the spine and cause a degree of spinal rigidity in some patients."
The review describes progression of contractures to the long finger flexors, shoulders, and spine.
Slow adult progression with late mobility loss
Age: Fourth decade onward
Adult decline may accelerate after age 40. Wheelchair use or outdoor mobility assistance does not necessarily mean loss of independent indoor walking. GeneReviews describes more than two thirds of those over 50 remaining independently ambulant indoors while needing outdoor support. In a separate 33-patient Bethlem cohort, five lost ambulation at a mean age of 44.6 years.
Show evidence (5 references)
PMID:20301676 SUPPORT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"The progression of weakness is slow, and more than two thirds of affected individuals older than age 50 years remain independently ambulatory indoors, while relying on supportive means for mobility outdoors."
GeneReviews describes the slow adult course and late mobility limitation.
PMID:10219778 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Due to slow but ongoing progression, more than two-thirds of patients over 50 years of age use a wheelchair."
The natural-history cohort quantifies wheelchair use after age 50.
PMID:37569848 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"In our BM group, only one third had a knee extension strength greater than 50% of the predicted value, while only one in ten showed similar retention of elbow flexion."
A contemporary 33-patient Bethlem cohort quantifies how much strength is actually lost, showing that mild does not mean trivial.

Differential Diagnoses

4

Conditions with similar clinical presentations that must be differentiated from Bethlem myopathy:

Emery-Dreifuss muscular dystrophy MONDO:0016830
Overlapping Features Early contractures and slowly progressive weakness overlap.
Distinguishing Features
  • Primary cardiac involvement is a major discriminator for Emery-Dreifuss disease, whereas cardiac review in Bethlem focuses on secondary consequences of hypoventilation.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:21691338 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"The presence of cardiac involvement clearly favors the presence of an Emery–Dreifuss muscular dystrophy, as primary cardiac involvement is not seen in the collagen VI-related myopathies."
Clinical differential diagnosis in the COL6 review.
Recessive calpainopathy Not Yet Curated MONDO:0009675
Overlapping Features The recessive CAPN3-related limb-girdle muscular dystrophy can resemble Bethlem disease through limb-girdle weakness and contractures. The source discusses calpainopathy more broadly; this term specifically represents its recessive form.
Distinguishing Features
  • Periscapular and biceps atrophy may suggest calpainopathy; muscle MRI patterns can overlap, so molecular diagnosis is needed.
Show evidence (1 reference)
url:https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sites/books/NBK1503/?report=reader SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"Of note, similar muscle imaging patterns may be seen in other conditions, including ... calpainopathy"
Imaging differential caveat.
COL12A1-related myopathy MONDO:0034022
Overlapping Features A genetically distinct myopathy/connective-tissue disorder can share hypotonia, laxity and contractures.
Distinguishing Features
  • COL12A1-related disease lies outside the COL6A1/COL6A2/COL6A3 Bethlem definition and is distinguished molecularly. Its overlapping connective-tissue features should not be treated as evidence for a COL6 variant.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:40309777 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Human Clinical
"Mild and non-progressive muscle weakness is common in genetic connective tissue diseases — for example, Marfan’s syndrome due to FBN1 pathogenic variants (4), osteogenesis imperfecta due to COL1A1 pathogenic variants (5), and Ehlers-Danlos/myopathy overlap due to COL12A1 pathogenic variants (6)."
Background clinical distinction in the mechanistic study introduction.
Ullrich congenital muscular dystrophy MONDO:0000355
Overlapping Features An allelic COL6-related dystrophy with overlapping matrix disease.
Distinguishing Features
  • More severe congenital weakness, earlier loss of walking and earlier ventilatory dependence distinguish UCMD; intermediate phenotypes lie between UCMD and adult-ambulant Bethlem disease.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:20301676 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"UCMD is characterized by congenital weakness, hypotonia, proximal joint contractures, and striking hyperlaxity of distal joints."
GeneReviews clinical distinction.

Clinical Trials

6
NCT01438788 PHASE_II COMPLETED
Completed uncontrolled low-protein-diet pilot: eight adults enrolled, seven completers (six Bethlem and one UCMD) analyzed in PMID27656840. Biomarker changes and exploratory outcomes do not establish controlled efficacy; loss of lean mass is relevant. Registry has no structured results posted, but a results paper is linked.

Experimental Models

3
COL6A1 exon-14-skipping patient fibroblasts PRIMARY_CELL_CULTURE
Mutant chains entered monomers but not dimers/tetramers, and mutant-containing molecules were not secreted. Normal collagen VI production was approximately halved. The engineered alpha3-chain deletion also studied in SaOS-2 cells was a separate construct, not this patient allele.
Cell source
Skin fibroblasts from the patient carrying the studied COL6A1 splice-donor allele
Pathograph links

Limitations: One structurally characterized splice allele; consequences need not match all dominant COL6 variants.

Direct assembly/secretion study of one patient exon-14-skipping allele, not a universal mechanism for all COL6 variants.

Animal Models

4
Col6a1-null mouse
This mild myopathic model supports mitochondrial, apoptotic and autophagy mechanisms, but a complete chain null differs from most dominant Bethlem alleles. Its skin is mechanically weaker with altered wound ultrastructure, yet pathological keloid-like scarring and overt wound-healing disturbance were not reproduced.
Species
Mus musculus
Genotype
Col6a1 homozygous knockout
Pathograph links

Limitations: Complete Col6a1 loss differs from most dominant structural Bethlem alleles; rescue of this mouse phenotype does not establish treatment efficacy in patients.

Bethlem-like col6a1 exon-13 morphant zebrafish
Transient exon-13 skipping, analogous to human COL6A1 exon 14, caused mild delayed motor and myotendinous/organellar abnormalities. The more severe exon-9 model was used for cyclosporin testing; its partial motor rescue cannot be claimed as a result in the mild arm. Morpholino embryos do not establish adult progression.

Col6a2-null mouse
The model develops weakness before overt fibrosis and abnormal matrix mechanics, with increased resting but disturbed post-injury TGF-beta activity. Disease was milder than human COL6 dystrophy and did not clearly progress through 60 weeks. An Ltbp4 binding-defective cross did not rescue strength, histology or physiology; no clinical treatment effect follows from this pathway finding.

Source YAML

click to show 
name: Bethlem myopathy
category: Mendelian
creation_date: "2026-07-31T00:00:00Z"
synonyms:
- Bethlem myopathy 1
- BTHLM1
- Bethlem muscular dystrophy
- Benign autosomal dominant myopathy
description: >-
  Bethlem myopathy is the usually milder, adult-ambulant end of the collagen VI-related dystrophy spectrum.
  Pathogenic COL6A1, COL6A2 or COL6A3 variants act through dominant or less commonly recessive mechanisms affecting
  collagen VI assembly, secretion or extracellular organization. Interstitial fibroblast-derived matrix dysfunction
  is linked to myofibre injury; autophagy, mitochondrial permeability and TGF-beta abnormalities are supported
  mainly by experimental models and selected patient studies, with incompletely defined intermediates. Slowly
  progressive proximal weakness and finger-flexor, elbow and Achilles contractures are characteristic, but
  onset and contractures vary and respiratory weakness can develop while walking is retained. Keloids, atrophic
  scars and follicular hyperkeratosis are useful connective-tissue clues. The historical label benign does
  not capture the possible adult functional decline.

  preferred_term: Bethlem myopathy
  term:

    label: Bethlem myopathy
parents:
- Collagen 6-related myopathy
- Congenital muscular dystrophy

  definition_type: OTHER
  description: >
    Bethlem muscular dystrophy is the mild end of the collagen VI-related
    dystrophy continuum and is characterized by the combination of proximal
    muscle weakness and joint contractures.

    snippet: >-
      In fact, in populations with a low degree of consanguinity, de novo autosomal-dominant mutations are
      the most common underlying mechanism of Ullrich CMD.1,30,32,80 Furthermore, although the typical genetic
      mechanism and pattern of inheritance for Bethlem myopathy is dominant, recessive mutations have also
      been seen in affected patients, albeit rarely.81,82 For example, the myosclerosis phenotype15 has emerged
      as a recessive form of Bethlem myopathy, in which a homozygous nonsense mutation in the COL6A2 gene results
      in a stable chain lacking the C2 domain but retaining the C1 domain,16 which presumably interferes with
      its ability to interact with other components in the extracellular matrix.
    explanation: The review documents a recessive (myosclerosis) form of Bethlem myopathy caused by a homozygous COL6A2 nonsense variant.

    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      Omissions of exon 16 in the α3(VI) chain account for the single most common mutation of this type, which
      is usually associated with a severe Ullrich CMD phenotype.32,80 Mutations that delete crucial cysteine
      residues in the collagenous triple helix domains of collagen VI subunits, as is the case for skipping
      of exon 14 in the α1(VI) chain, seem to have a more limited dominant-negative effect that shifts the
      clinical phenotype of collagen VI-related myopathy towards the mild end of the spectrum.30,80 Indeed,
      exon-14-skipping mutations are the single most common mutation type seen in patients with Bethlem myopathy.30,85,88,91–93
      In-frame exon-skipping mutations located in the C-terminal end of the collagenous triple-helical domain
      interfere with assembly of the three peptide subunits into collagen VI monomers, with the consequence
      that abnormal peptides are prevented from assembly into tetramers.
    explanation: The review identifies COL6A1 exon 14 skipping as the most common Bethlem mutation type.

progression:
- phase: Variable onset from birth to adulthood
  age_range: Prenatal life to adulthood
  notes: >-
    Onset is variable. The 1999 seven-kindred series found manifestations in nearly all affected children by
    age two, whereas the 2023 cohort included 10 of 33 with symptoms at birth, 11 in early childhood, two in
    adolescence, six first noticing difficulty in early adulthood and four initially asymptomatic. These ascertainment-specific
    distributions are not contradictory universal age cutoffs.

    independently ambulant indoors while needing outdoor support. In a separate 33-patient Bethlem cohort,
    five lost ambulation at a mean age of 44.6 years.

genetic:
- name: COL6A1
  gene_term:
    preferred_term: COL6A1
    term:
      id: hgnc:2211
      label: COL6A1
  association: Causative
  relationship_type: CAUSATIVE
  variant_origin: GERMLINE
  features: >-
    COL6A1 encodes the collagen VI alpha 1 chain. Structural variants including glycine substitutions and in-frame
    exon 14 skipping cause Bethlem disease. For one splice-donor allele, patient RNA showed an 18-amino-acid
    in-frame deletion and fibroblast studies found blocked dimer/tetramer assembly and secretion of mutant-containing
    molecules. Exon 14 skipping was associated with worse adult evolution in one cohort; phenotype cannot be
    predicted reliably from the broad variant class alone.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:15689448
    reference_title: "Automated genomic sequence analysis of the three collagen VI genes: applications to Ullrich congenital muscular dystrophy and Bethlem myopathy."

    snippet: >-

      myopathy (BM) and Ullrich congenital muscular dystrophy (UCMD).
    explanation: Sequencing evidence establishes the three collagen VI genes as Bethlem myopathy causes.

  - *id001
- name: COL6A2
  gene_term:
    preferred_term: COL6A2
    term:
      id: hgnc:2212
      label: COL6A2
  association: Causative
  relationship_type: CAUSATIVE
  variant_origin: GERMLINE
  features: >-
    COL6A2 can cause dominant structural-variant disease or recessive Bethlem phenotypes. In one family, p.Gln889*
    and p.Pro260_Lys261insProPro in trans were associated with reduced normal protein and impaired secretion/assembly,
    respectively; carrier parents were asymptomatic. In a separate three-sister family, c.1514G>A (p.Gly505Asp)
    and c.2877G>C (p.Glu959Asp) segregated in trans, and proband fibroblasts showed intracellular retention
    and absent extracellular network. Those two missense variants were initially uncertain; combined segregation
    and functional findings supported the authors' causal interpretation, without separate assays proving the
    effect of each allele. Myosclerosis is an allelic, clinically distinct presentation.
  evidence:

    explanation: The original study characterizes the Gly-X-Y disrupting substitutions found in Bethlem kindreds.

pathophysiology:
- name: Collagen VI alpha-chain gene defects
  biological_scale: MOLECULAR
  description: >-
    Pathogenic COL6A1, COL6A2 or COL6A3 variants alter the obligatory collagen VI chains. Dominant structural
    alleles and recessive residual-function combinations can cause Bethlem myopathy. Their consequences depend
    on the affected domain and assembly step: entry into a monomer does not imply that mutant protein reaches
    tetramers or the extracellular matrix. Ordinary heterozygous loss-of-function alleles can be clinically
    tolerated; pathogenic structural alleles should not be equated with simple gene dosage reduction.

- name: Defective intracellular collagen VI assembly
  biological_scale: MOLECULAR
  description: >-
    Variant-specific defects impair intracellular collagen VI oligomerization. For the studied COL6A1 exon-14-skipping
    allele, mutant chains enter heterotrimeric monomers but fail to form dimers and tetramers. Other assembly-competent
    dominant alleles may instead interfere with later extracellular organization; the one-in-sixteen normal-tetramer
    argument is not applicable to every Bethlem allele.
  evidence:

- name: Endomysial collagen VI network dysfunction
  biological_scale: TISSUE
  description: >-
    Collagen VI is supplied to muscle by interstitial fibroblasts. Variant-dependent defects affect extracellular
    matrix organization or function, but routine muscle staining can be normal in Bethlem disease, including
    preserved colocalization with basal-lamina perlecan in 13 of 15 dominant cases in a 2023 series. Normal
    staining does not prove normal molecular interactions; conversely, basement-membrane mislocalization is
    not a universal Bethlem finding.

  biological_scale: CELLULAR
  description: >-
    Abnormal collagen VI interactions may compromise matrix-to-cell coupling and force transmission. Biglycan
    and the dystrophin-associated complex were proposed partners in the earlier review, but the specific interaction
    defect is not demonstrated in every Bethlem patient. Human ultrastructural abnormalities and mouse mechanical
    measurements support matrix dysfunction without establishing a universal receptor-to-mitochondria signaling
    chain.
  cell_types:
  - preferred_term: skeletal muscle fiber
    term:
      id: CL:0008002
      label: skeletal muscle fiber
  biological_processes:
  - preferred_term: cell-matrix adhesion
    modifier: DECREASED

      id: GO:0007160
      label: cell-matrix adhesion
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:8782832
    reference_title: Type VI collagen mutations in Bethlem myopathy, an autosomal dominant myopathy with contractures.
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: >-
      Among the diverse family of collagens, the widely expressed microfibrillar type VI collagen is believed
      to play a role in bridging cells with the extracellular matrix.
    explanation: States the anchoring function of collagen VI whose loss is proposed as the Bethlem mechanism.
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    directness: INDIRECT
  - reference: PMID:21691338
    reference_title: "The collagen VI-related myopathies: muscle meets its matrix."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: >-
      Since collagen VI binds to biglycan,55 which interacts with the sarcoglycan and dystroglycan complex,56,57
      collagen VI might be indirectly linked to muscle cell surface receptors via biglycan and the dystrophin-associated
      protein complex.
    explanation: Proposes the molecular route linking the collagen VI network to the sarcolemma.
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    directness: INDIRECT
  - reference: PMID:21691338
    reference_title: "The collagen VI-related myopathies: muscle meets its matrix."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: >-
      A mouse model of collagen VI deficiency has been generated by knockout of the collagen VI Col6a1 locus,
      resulting in a complete absence of collagen VI expression in muscle.69 In contrast to the human disease,
      these mice only show a mild neuromuscular disorder without much overt weakness.69 Muscles from these
      animals also show an increased incidence of apoptosis, which correlates with facilitated breakdown of
      the potential across the mitochondrial permeability pore when primed with oligomycin.65 This effect was
      preventable by inactivation of cyclophilin D using ciclosporin, its derivative Debio0025,70–72 or genetic
      inactivation of cyclophilin D.73 These interventions also resulted in decreased apoptosis and improved
      physiological functioning of isolated muscles from the collagen VI-knockout animals.65,72 Subsequent
      work using the same model suggests that impairments in autophagocytic flux (including impaired mitochondrial
      autophagy) also occur in the muscle cells of collagen VI-deficient mice, and possibly exacerbate the
      accumulation of defective mitochondria.74 Attachment of cells to the extracellular matrix is important
      for preventing apoptosis in general,75 which could be particularly relevant for muscle disorders that
      directly involve interactions between matrix and muscle, as is the case for both collagen VI-related
      and LAMA2-related CMDs.76–78 Work in cell cultures has shown that collagen VI-deficient cells exhibit
      decreased adherence to their surroundings,60,79 emphasizing the importance of this muscle cell–extracellular
      matrix interface.
    explanation: Cell-culture data support reduced matrix adherence, although the precise myofibre receptor remains unidentified.
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    directness: INDIRECT
  - reference: PMID:36982625
    reference_title: Extracellular Matrix Disorganization and Sarcolemmal Alterations in COL6-Related Myopathy Patients with New Variants of COL6 Genes.
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "It localizes at the cell surface, creating a microfilamentous network that links the cytoskeleton to the extracellular matrix."
    explanation: States the cell-surface bridging role of collagen VI whose loss defines this node.
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    directness: INDIRECT
  downstream:

- name: Myofibre mitochondrial dysfunction and permeability transition pore dysregulation
  biological_scale: CELLULAR
  description: >-
    Col6a1-null mouse myofibres have abnormal mitochondrial and sarcoplasmic-reticulum ultrastructure and latent
    permeability-transition susceptibility. Cyclosporin A or genetic cyclophilin-D ablation rescues experimental
    abnormalities. Muscle biopsies in a short open human pilot also showed mitochondrial dysfunction and increased
    apoptosis. These selected COL6-disease observations do not establish identical abnormalities in every Bethlem
    patient or clinical benefit from pore modulation.

- name: Progressive myofibre degeneration

  description: >-
    Myofibre injury and loss produce progressive proximal-predominant weakness and variable enzyme leakage
    and myopathic electromyography. Functional decline can occur over decades, and wheelchair use for longer
    distances is distinct from complete loss of independent walking. Fibrotic replacement is a related, separately
    represented tissue process.

- name: Tendon and periarticular connective tissue involvement

- name: Dermal extracellular matrix dysfunction
  biological_scale: TISSUE
  description: >-
    Collagen VI dysfunction affects dermal matrix organization and is associated with follicular hyperkeratosis,
    soft skin and abnormal scarring. The detailed route to each skin manifestation remains uncertain. Col6a1-null
    mice have mechanically weaker skin and altered wound ultrastructure but do not reproduce the human keloid
    phenotype or an overt wound-healing delay.

- name: Dysregulated extracellular TGF-beta bioavailability
  description: >-
    Col6a2-null mice have excessive resting TGF-beta activity, but a blunted early release after injury and
    relatively prolonged later activity. Protein-complex and fibroblast reporter assays implicate altered extracellular
    sequestration or activation without increased total ligand transcription or protein. This is a COL6 model
    mechanism, not a demonstrated universal Bethlem patient defect. Disrupting Ltbp4-TGF-beta binding did not
    rescue the mouse phenotype.
  biological_scale: TISSUE
  evidence:

    supports: SUPPORT

      without an increase in total TGF-β transcription or translation.
    explanation: >-

diagnosis:
- name: Muscle collagen VI immunohistochemistry
  diagnosis_term:
    preferred_term: diagnostic procedure
    term:
      id: NCIT:C18020
      label: Diagnostic Procedure
  description: >-
    Collagen VI abundance and colocalization with a basement-membrane marker can be normal in Bethlem disease:
    13 of 15 dominant cases in one series retained a normal perlecan-overlapping pattern. Thus a normal stain
    does not exclude the diagnosis. Abnormal staining can support COL6 disease but is not a universal requirement,
    and expression may vary with age.
  results: Retained but mislocalized collagen VI, away from the basement membrane, supports dominant collagen VI disease.

- name: COL6A1, COL6A2, and COL6A3 molecular genetic testing
  diagnosis_term:
    preferred_term: genetic testing
    term:
      id: NCIT:C15709
      label: Genetic Testing
  description: >-
    Diagnosis requires compatible clinical findings and pathogenic or likely pathogenic COL6A1, COL6A2 or COL6A3
    variant(s), with inheritance and segregation assessed. Testing can use a multigene panel or exome/genome
    sequencing and selected deletion/duplication or RNA studies when standard sequencing is unrevealing. The
    62% putative-variant yield in a 2005 coding-exon study is historical and should not be presented as modern
    sensitivity. Variants of uncertain significance neither confirm nor exclude disease; functional evidence
    must be interpreted with appropriate controls. Deep-intronic COL6A1 testing is relevant to the wider COL6
    diagnostic spectrum, without labeling its typical severe phenotype as Bethlem.
  results: A heterozygous or biallelic COL6A1, COL6A2, or COL6A3 pathogenic variant confirms the diagnosis.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:20301676
    reference_title: Collagen VI-Related Dystrophies.
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "The diagnosis can be confirmed by identification of a heterozygous or biallelic pathogenic variant(s) in COL6A1, COL6A2, or COL6A3."
    explanation: GeneReviews defines the molecular confirmation criterion.
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS

    Physical therapy and occupational therapy provide recommendations for joint

    severe or asymmetric shortening impairs gait. Surgery disrupting other joint capsules is generally discouraged
    because fixed postoperative contractures and loss of motion can result. Scoliosis surgery requires coordinated
    pulmonary, anesthesia, intensive-care and orthopedic planning.

- name: Nocturnal noninvasive ventilation
  description: >-
    Respiratory support is guided by upright/supine pulmonary testing and polysomnography, including carbon-dioxide
    monitoring. GeneReviews recommends initiation of nocturnal BiPAP when FVC is 60% predicted or lower, with

- name: Respiratory, cardiac, and orthopedic surveillance
  description: >-
    At least annual upright and supine FVC, sleep evaluation for hypoventilation, clinical/radiographic scoliosis
    assessment and rehabilitation review are recommended for Bethlem disease. Initial pulmonary testing begins
    in childhood, around age five when feasible. GeneReviews recommends annual ECG/echocardiography to assess
    secondary right-heart strain from inadequate ventilation; this does not imply primary cardiomyopathy is
    a characteristic Bethlem feature. Mobility-device needs and joint function should be reassessed over time.

- name: Genetic counseling
  description: >-
    A person with an autosomal dominant pathogenic variant has a 50% transmission risk in each pregnancy; sibling
    risk is 50% when a parent is heterozygous. Parental somatic/germline mosaicism can complicate apparently
    de novo families, and clinical severity varies within families. For recessive disease with two carrier
    parents, each pregnancy has a 25% affected, 50% carrier and 25% noncarrier chance. Once familial variants
    are established, prenatal and preimplantation genetic testing are possible.
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: Genetic Counseling
    term:
      id: NCIT:C15240
      label: Genetic Counseling

- name: Cyclophilin D and permeability transition pore modulation
  description: >-
    Cyclosporin A is investigational for COL6 myopathy. A one-month open pilot in five patients (four UCMD
    and one Bethlem) showed improved mitochondrial and apoptotic biopsy readouts, without establishing functional
    benefit or long-term Bethlem efficacy. Genetic cyclophilin-D ablation supports the target in Col6a1-null
    mice. Immunosuppression and toxicity preclude treating a biopsy response as a routine-care recommendation.

- name: Investigational low-protein diet for autophagy modulation
  description: >-
    A one-year open, uncontrolled pilot enrolled eight adults and analyzed seven completers (six Bethlem, one
    UCMD). Muscle autophagy-associated markers increased and apoptosis decreased, but human flux was not directly
    quantified. The six ambulant Bethlem patients improved on the four-stair test; six-minute walk, ten-meter
    walk, strength and the mixed-group FVC change were not statistically significant. Participants lost about
    9% body weight and 7% lean mass despite preservation of the lean-to-fat percentage. The diet remains investigational
    and requires nutritional oversight; the study does not establish long-term clinical efficacy.

- name: Individualized moderate aerobic exercise
  description: >-
    Supervised individualized moderate exercise can complement stretching and mobility support. In a ten-week
    home-training study, four men improved peak oxygen uptake by 16% compared with baseline, while two controls
    did not improve. CK remained stable and functional tests did not change significantly. The small study
    supports feasibility and aerobic conditioning, without establishing long-term disease modification or safety
    of intense eccentric loading.

discussions:
- discussion_id: disc_bethlem_col6a1_null_model_fidelity
  kind: HUMAN_MODEL_MISMATCH
  status: OPEN
  prompt: >
    Does the Col6a1-null mouse, which lacks collagen VI entirely, faithfully
    model dominant-negative Bethlem myopathy, in which an abnormal collagen VI
    network is still present in the matrix?
  attaches_to:
  - pathophysiology#Myofibre mitochondrial dysfunction and permeability transition pore dysregulation
  - pathophysiology#Defective autophagic clearance of damaged organelles
  rationale: >-
    Complete Col6a1-null mice reproduce selected mitochondrial and autophagy defects but differ from human
    dominant structural variants. Many dominant Bethlem muscle biopsies have normal collagen VI localization
    as well as preserved signal. Neither normal staining nor a null model resolves the exact molecular dysfunction
    in every patient. Genotype-stratified functional assays are needed to assess transferability of therapeutic
    mechanisms.
  proposed_experiments:
  - experiment_id: exp_bethlem_dominant_negative_ptp_flux
    name: Permeability transition and autophagic flux by COL6 genotype class
    description: >
      Measure permeability transition pore sensitivity and autophagic flux in
      myofibres or myoblasts from genotyped patients carrying dominant-negative
      COL6A1 exon 14 skipping, and compare against recessive collagen VI null
      patients and unaffected controls.
  - experiment_id: exp_bethlem_knockin_mouse
    name: Bethlem dominant-negative knock-in mouse
    description: >
      Generate a knock-in mouse carrying a Bethlem dominant-negative Col6a1
      in-frame exon deletion and compare its mitochondrial, autophagic, and
      contracture phenotype with the existing Col6a1-null mouse.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:21691338
    reference_title: "The collagen VI-related myopathies: muscle meets its matrix."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "In contrast to the human disease, these mice only show a mild neuromuscular disorder without much overt weakness."
    explanation: >
      The review states directly that the Col6a1-null mouse phenotype is milder
      than human disease, which is the translational mismatch at issue.
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
  - reference: PMID:37569848
    reference_title: 'New Clinical and Immunofluoresence Data of Collagen VI-Related Myopathy: A Single Center Cohort of 69 Patients.'
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    directness: DIRECT
    snippet: >-
      Muscle biopsies from 13 out of 15 BM patients with dominant mutations exhibited a normal collagen VI
      pattern, as evidenced by the continuous overlap with the anti-perlecan staining (Table 2).
    explanation: >-
      Normal abundance and basal-lamina colocalization in most dominant Bethlem biopsies in this series; a
      normal result does not exclude disease.
  posed_date: "2026-07-31T00:00:00Z"
- discussion_id: disc_bethlem_matrix_sarcolemma_receptor
  kind: KNOWLEDGE_GAP
  status: OPEN
  prompt: >
    Which molecular partner mediates the interaction of collagen VI with the
    muscle basement membrane and links the microfibrillar network to the
    sarcolemma?
  attaches_to:
  - pathophysiology#Impaired matrix-to-sarcolemma anchoring of the myofibre
  rationale: >-
    The matrix-to-myofibre link remains incompletely resolved. The 2025 Col6a2-null study identified collagen
    VI-associated proteins and altered TGF-beta-containing complexes, but co-immunoprecipitation does not prove
    direct pairwise binding or identify a universal receptor pathway in human Bethlem disease. Whether altered
    mechanical coupling, extracellular growth-factor availability and mitochondrial/autophagy pathways act
    in sequence or partly in parallel remains unresolved.
  proposed_experiments:
  - experiment_id: exp_bethlem_basement_membrane_interactome
    name: Muscle basement membrane collagen VI interactome
    description: >
      Perform proximity labeling or crosslinking mass spectrometry on the muscle
      basement membrane to identify the collagen VI binding partners present at
      the myofibre surface.

    name: Biglycan and dystroglycan complex phenocopy test
    description: >
      Test whether disruption of biglycan or of the dystrophin-associated protein
      complex phenocopies the collagen VI mitochondrial defect in otherwise
      wild-type myofibres.

  - reference: PMID:21691338
    reference_title: "The collagen VI-related myopathies: muscle meets its matrix."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: >-
      Additional data suggest that the increased opening of mitochondrial permeability transition pores is
      not specific to the collagen VI-related disorders, but can also be seen in other neuromuscular disorders,103
      which would mean that the treatment approach outlined above would be applicable in other muscle disorders,
      as has already been suggested for the δ-sarcoglycan-deficient and dystrophin-deficient mouse models.104
      A crucial step will be to establish exactly how collagen VI deficiency in the extracellular matrix leaves
      muscle cells vulnerable to apoptosis; that is, how the presence of normal collagen VI in the extracellular
      matrix contributes to the health of myofibers.
    explanation: The review names this as the crucial unresolved mechanistic step.
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS

    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
    directness: DIRECT
    snippet: >-
      Together, these findings suggest that absence of collagen VI from the muscle ECM increases active TGF-β
      without an increase in total TGF-β transcription or translation.
    explanation: >-
      Col6a2-null mouse tissue and fibroblast assays; altered activation rather than increased overall production.
  posed_date: "2026-07-31T00:00:00Z"
notes: >-
  This entry covers COL6A1/COL6A2/COL6A3-related Bethlem myopathy (MONDO:0008029), including its three gene-defined
  subtypes. COL12A1-related Bethlem myopathy 2 is a separate disorder. Bethlem, intermediate COL6-related dystrophy
  and Ullrich congenital muscular dystrophy form a severity continuum, but cohort results and treatment outcomes
  should retain their clinical subgroup. Historical benign terminology is retained as a synonym only; adult
  progression and respiratory impairment can be clinically significant. Molecular diagnosis requires interpretation
  of the variant, segregation and phenotype rather than accepting every rare COL6 variant as causal.
references:
- reference: PMID:20301676
  title: Collagen VI-Related Dystrophies.
  tags:
  - GeneReviews
  findings:
  - statement: >
      GeneReviews defines Bethlem muscular dystrophy as the mild end of the
      collagen VI-related dystrophy continuum, characterized by proximal muscle
      weakness plus contractures of the elbows, Achilles tendons, and long finger
      flexors, with inconsistent respiratory involvement, and provides the
      management and surveillance recommendations used in this entry.
    supporting_text: "Bethlem muscular dystrophy is characterized by a combination of proximal muscle weakness and joint contractures."
- reference: PMID:8782832
  title: Type VI collagen mutations in Bethlem myopathy, an autosomal dominant myopathy with contractures.
  findings:

      The original gene-identification study linked Bethlem myopathy to the
      COL6A1-COL6A2 cluster and COL6A3, and identified Gly-X-Y disrupting
      substitutions in the triple-helical domain.
    supporting_text: "Both mutations disrupt the Gly-X-Y motif of the triple helical domain by substitution of Gly for either Val or Ser."

  title: "The collagen VI-related myopathies: muscle meets its matrix."

      The definitive review covers collagen VI assembly, the non-cell-autonomous

      contracture and skin phenotype, muscle imaging, prevalence, and the
      genotype-phenotype gradient across the COL6-RD spectrum.
    supporting_text: "In northern England, the prevalence of Ullrich CMD is 0.13 cases per 100,000 of the population, and that of Bethlem myopathy is 0.77 cases per 100,000."
- reference: PMID:14625552
  title: Mitochondrial dysfunction and apoptosis in myopathic mice with collagen VI deficiency.
  findings:
  - statement: >
      The Col6a1-null mouse, whose muscle phenotype resembles Bethlem myopathy,
      revealed latent mitochondrial dysfunction, permeability transition pore
      dysregulation, and spontaneous myofibre apoptosis reversible by collagen VI
      replating or cyclosporin A.
    supporting_text: "We previously generated collagen VI-deficient (Col6a1-/-) mice and showed that they have a muscle phenotype that strongly resembles Bethlem myopathy."
- reference: PMID:19293339
  title: Genetic ablation of cyclophilin D rescues mitochondrial defects and prevents muscle apoptosis in collagen VI myopathic mice.
  findings:
  - statement: >-
      Cyclophilin-D ablation rescued mitochondrial and apoptotic abnormalities in Col6a1-null mice, supporting
      an experimental target without establishing human clinical efficacy.
    supporting_text: >-
      In the absence of Cyp-D, Col6a1(-/-) mice show negligible myofiber degeneration, rescue from mitochondrial
      dysfunction and ultrastructural defects, and normalized incidence of apoptosis.
- reference: PMID:21037586
  title: Autophagy is defective in collagen VI muscular dystrophies, and its reactivation rescues myofiber degeneration.
  findings:
  - statement: >
      Defective autophagy underlies the persistence of abnormal organelles and
      apoptosis in collagen VI-deficient muscle, and reduced beclin-1 and Bnip3
      are demonstrable in Bethlem myopathy patient muscle.
    supporting_text: "Furthermore, muscle biopsies from subjects with Bethlem myopathy or Ullrich congenital muscular dystrophy had reduced protein amounts of beclin-1 and Bnip3."
- reference: PMID:18362356
  title: Cyclosporin A corrects mitochondrial dysfunction and muscle apoptosis in patients with collagen VI myopathies.
  findings:
  - statement: >
      An open pilot trial in five patients showed that oral cyclosporin A
      normalized mitochondrial dysfunction and apoptosis in muscle biopsies,
      providing human proof of mechanism for permeability transition pore
      modulation.
    supporting_text: >-
      Both of these pathologic signs were largely normalized after 1 month of oral cyclosporin A administration,
      which also increased muscle regeneration.

    term:
      id: MONDO:0016830
      label: Emery-Dreifuss muscular dystrophy
  description: >-
    Early contractures and slowly progressive weakness overlap.
  distinguishing_features:
  - Primary cardiac involvement is a major discriminator for Emery-Dreifuss disease, whereas cardiac review in Bethlem focuses on secondary consequences of hypoventilation.
  evidence:

      The presence of cardiac involvement clearly favors the presence of an Emery–Dreifuss muscular dystrophy,
      as primary cardiac involvement is not seen in the collagen VI-related myopathies.
    explanation: >-
      Clinical differential diagnosis in the COL6 review.

    The recessive CAPN3-related limb-girdle muscular dystrophy can resemble Bethlem disease through limb-girdle
    weakness and contractures. The source discusses calpainopathy more broadly; this term specifically represents
    its recessive form.

    term:
      id: MONDO:0034022
      label: Bethlem myopathy 2
  description: >-
    A genetically distinct myopathy/connective-tissue disorder can share hypotonia, laxity and contractures.
  distinguishing_features:
  - COL12A1-related disease lies outside the COL6A1/COL6A2/COL6A3 Bethlem definition and is distinguished molecularly. Its overlapping connective-tissue features should not be treated as evidence for a COL6 variant.

      Marfan’s syndrome due to FBN1 pathogenic variants (4), osteogenesis imperfecta due to COL1A1 pathogenic
      variants (5), and Ehlers-Danlos/myopathy overlap due to COL12A1 pathogenic variants (6).
    explanation: >-
      Background clinical distinction in the mechanistic study introduction.

    yet pathological keloid-like scarring and overt wound-healing disturbance were not reproduced.

References & Deep Research

30
PMID:20301676
Collagen VI-Related Dystrophies.
1 finding
GeneReviews defines Bethlem muscular dystrophy as the mild end of the collagen VI-related dystrophy continuum, characterized by proximal muscle weakness plus contractures of the elbows, Achilles tendons, and long finger flexors, with inconsistent respiratory involvement, and provides the management and surveillance recommendations used in this entry.
"Bethlem muscular dystrophy is characterized by a combination of proximal muscle weakness and joint contractures."
PMID:8782832
Type VI collagen mutations in Bethlem myopathy, an autosomal dominant myopathy with contractures.
1 finding
The original gene-identification study linked Bethlem myopathy to the COL6A1-COL6A2 cluster and COL6A3, and identified Gly-X-Y disrupting substitutions in the triple-helical domain.
"Both mutations disrupt the Gly-X-Y motif of the triple helical domain by substitution of Gly for either Val or Ser."
PMID:10219778
Bethlem myopathy: a slowly progressive congenital muscular dystrophy with contractures.
1 finding
The seven-kindred natural-history study documents early presentation in the studied children and later wheelchair use, which does not necessarily imply complete loss of indoor ambulation.
"Due to slow but ongoing progression, more than two-thirds of patients over 50 years of age use a wheelchair."
PMID:15689448
Automated genomic sequence analysis of the three collagen VI genes: applications to Ullrich congenital muscular dystrophy and Bethlem myopathy.
1 finding
In 2005, coding-exon sequencing identified putative COL6 variants in 62% of 79 selected UCMD/Bethlem patients. This historical yield is not the sensitivity of contemporary diagnostic testing.
"We found putative mutations in one of the COL6 genes in 62% of patients."
PMID:21691338
The collagen VI-related myopathies: muscle meets its matrix.
1 finding
The definitive review covers collagen VI assembly, the non-cell-autonomous interstitial fibroblast origin of muscle collagen VI, the Bethlem contracture and skin phenotype, muscle imaging, prevalence, and the genotype-phenotype gradient across the COL6-RD spectrum.
"In northern England, the prevalence of Ullrich CMD is 0.13 cases per 100,000 of the population, and that of Bethlem myopathy is 0.77 cases per 100,000."
PMID:14625552
Mitochondrial dysfunction and apoptosis in myopathic mice with collagen VI deficiency.
1 finding
The Col6a1-null mouse, whose muscle phenotype resembles Bethlem myopathy, revealed latent mitochondrial dysfunction, permeability transition pore dysregulation, and spontaneous myofibre apoptosis reversible by collagen VI replating or cyclosporin A.
"We previously generated collagen VI-deficient (Col6a1-/-) mice and showed that they have a muscle phenotype that strongly resembles Bethlem myopathy."
PMID:19293339
Genetic ablation of cyclophilin D rescues mitochondrial defects and prevents muscle apoptosis in collagen VI myopathic mice.
1 finding
Cyclophilin-D ablation rescued mitochondrial and apoptotic abnormalities in Col6a1-null mice, supporting an experimental target without establishing human clinical efficacy.
"In the absence of Cyp-D, Col6a1(-/-) mice show negligible myofiber degeneration, rescue from mitochondrial dysfunction and ultrastructural defects, and normalized incidence of apoptosis."
PMID:21037586
Autophagy is defective in collagen VI muscular dystrophies, and its reactivation rescues myofiber degeneration.
1 finding
Defective autophagy underlies the persistence of abnormal organelles and apoptosis in collagen VI-deficient muscle, and reduced beclin-1 and Bnip3 are demonstrable in Bethlem myopathy patient muscle.
"Furthermore, muscle biopsies from subjects with Bethlem myopathy or Ullrich congenital muscular dystrophy had reduced protein amounts of beclin-1 and Bnip3."
PMID:18362356
Cyclosporin A corrects mitochondrial dysfunction and muscle apoptosis in patients with collagen VI myopathies.
1 finding
An open pilot trial in five patients showed that oral cyclosporin A normalized mitochondrial dysfunction and apoptosis in muscle biopsies, providing human proof of mechanism for permeability transition pore modulation.
"Both of these pathologic signs were largely normalized after 1 month of oral cyclosporin A administration, which also increased muscle regeneration."
PMID:24339618
Bethlem myopathy: An autosomal dominant myopathy with flexion contractures, keloids, and follicular hyperkeratosis.
1 finding
A clinically diagnosed Indian family had contractures, keloids and follicular hyperkeratosis. The father had normal CK and collagen VI immunostaining; sons had raised CK. Molecular testing was not performed.
"All had flexion contractures, keloids, and follicular hyperkeratosis without muscle hypertrophy."
PMID:25158062
Col6a1 null mice as a model to study skin phenotypes in patients with collagen VI related myopathies: expression of classical and novel collagen VI variants during wound healing.
1 finding
Col6a1-null mice have mechanically weaker skin and altered wound ultrastructure, but no overt wound-healing delay or recreation of human keloids.
"However, in contrast to the hypertrophic scars or keloids occurring in patients with a collagen VI myopathies, we did not observe such disturbed wound healing in the Col6a1 null mice."
PMID:37569848
New Clinical and Immunofluoresence Data of Collagen VI-Related Myopathy: A Single Center Cohort of 69 Patients.
1 finding
A 69-patient single-center collagen VI cohort including 33 Bethlem myopathy patients quantifies residual strength, and shows that collagen VI immunofluorescence correlates with severity but increases with age within an individual.
"In our BM group, only one third had a knee extension strength greater than 50% of the predicted value, while only one in ten showed similar retention of elbow flexion."
PMID:36982625
Extracellular Matrix Disorganization and Sarcolemmal Alterations in COL6-Related Myopathy Patients with New Variants of COL6 Genes.
1 finding
A mixed COL6-phenotype biopsy series reports extracellular and sarcolemmal abnormalities. Several variants remain uncertain or were predicted benign in the study tables; morphology does not independently establish pathogenicity for each variant.
"they documented the high variability in COL6 distribution and the extracellular matrix disorganization"
clinicaltrials:NCT01438788
Low Protein Diet to Correct Defective Autophagy in Patients With Collagen VI Related Myopathies
1 finding
A phase II open-label pilot tested a normocaloric low-protein diet in eight adults with Bethlem myopathy or Ullrich congenital muscular dystrophy to reactivate autophagy, with muscle beclin-1 as the primary endpoint.
"The primary end point of the study will be the change in muscle biopsy of Beclin 1, a marker of autophagy, at 1 year of LPD treatment when compared to baseline."
PMID:21078917

"Physical therapy should be focused on the maintenance of function and mobility, prevention or treatment of joint contractures and spine deformities"
